## RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages) -

Investments made into GGEF are subject to market fluctuations and subject to the risks inherent in all investments. Accordingly, no assurance can be given that the investment objective of GGEF will be achieved. The risk factors related to GGEF can be categorized as fund risks and as risks related to investing in emerging markets and GGEF's sectors of focus. No exhaustive list of these risks can be given, a detailed list is available in section VI of GGEF's private placement memorandum (PPM).

Specific risk factors are continuously assessed throughout the entire investment process and are strictly monitored. Appropriate mitigation strategies are analyzed and implemented proactively from the earliest stage possible.

| Selected Risk Factor 1 Development   | Cycle Risk   |        |
|--------------------------------------|--------------|--------|
| Category                             | Probability  | Impact |
| Technical and operational            | Medium       | Medium |

## Description

Early-stage  and  development-stage  companies  often  experience  unexpected  problems  in  the  areas  of  product development,  manufacturing,  marketing,  financing  and  general  management,  which,  in  some  cases,  cannot  be adequately  solved.  In  addition,  such  companies  may  require  substantial  amounts  of  financing  which  may  not  be available through institutional private placements or the public markets. The percentage of companies that survive and prosper may be small.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Invest in projects with shorter build-out cycle
-  Lock in capital cost and hedge currency
-  Key development risks to be tied up at the beginning of the contract - Land, Permits etc.
-  Leverage Everstone and LSBP relationships for business arrangements including JVs

## Selected Risk Factor 2 Capital Risk

| Category   | Probability   | Impact   |
|------------|---------------|----------|
| Credit     | Low           | Medium   |

## Description

The Portfolio Companies can be expected to require additional financing to satisfy their working capital requirements or acquisition strategies. The amount of additional financing needed will depend upon the maturity and objectives of the particular Portfolio Company. Each round of financing (whether from GGEF or other investors) is typically intended to provide a Portfolio Company with enough capital to reach the next major valuation milestone. If the funds provided are not sufficient, such Portfolio Company may have to raise additional capital at a price unfavorable to the existing investors  in  such  Portfolio  Company,  including  GGEF.  In  addition,  GGEF  may  make  additional  debt  and  equity investments or exercise warrants, options or convertible securities that were acquired in the initial investment in such Portfolio Company in order to preserve GGEF's proportionate ownership when a subsequent financing is planned or to protect the assets of GGEF when such Portfolio Company's performance does not meet expectations. The availability of capital is generally a function of capital market conditions that are beyond the control of GGEF or any Portfolio Company. There can be no assurance that the Portfolio Companies will be able to predict accurately the future capital requirements necessary for success or that additional funds will be available from any source, or available at prices that are favorable to the Portfolio Company.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Leverage GGEF's Sponsors and investors network to raise capital for investee companies

|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Selected Risk Factor 3 Environmental, Social and Governance Risk                           | Selected Risk Factor 3 Environmental, Social and Governance Risk                           | Selected Risk Factor 3 Environmental, Social and Governance Risk                           |
| Category                                                                                   | Probability                                                                                | Impact                                                                                     |
| Governance                                                                                 | Medium                                                                                     | Medium                                                                                     |

## Description

There can be no guarantee that all costs and risks regarding compliance with environmental laws and regulations can be identified. Moreover, failure to comply with any such requirements could have a material adverse effect on a Portfolio Company  and  there  can  be  no  assurance  that  Portfolio  Companies  will  at  all  times  comply  with  all  applicable environmental laws, regulations and permit requirements. Past practices or future operations of Portfolio Companies could  also  result  in  material  personal  injury  or  property  damage  claims.  Any  noncompliance  with  these  laws  and regulations could subject the Fund and its Portfolio Companies to material administrative, civil or criminal penalties or other liabilities. The Fund may experience material losses due to these risks.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Apply EverSource's ESG framework
-  Align companies to impact assessment framework
-  Training of employees to sustain strong ESG practices

| Selected Risk Factor 4 Technology / Innovation Risk   | Selected Risk Factor 4 Technology / Innovation Risk   | Selected Risk Factor 4 Technology / Innovation Risk   |
|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                              | Probability                                           | Impact                                                |
| Technical and operational                             | Medium                                                | Medium                                                |

## Description

A  portion  of  the  GGEF  Assets  may  be  invested  in  companies  in  highly  competitive  markets  or  product  segments dominated by firms with substantially greater financial and technical resources than the Portfolio Companies in which GGEF invests. Portfolio Companies in which GGEF invests may operate in product segments that face technological changes and/or may be dominated by other firms or organizations. These and other inherent business risks could affect the performance of the Portfolio Companies and affect the value of Fund equity investments, thereby affecting GGEF as a whole due to its involvement in Portfolio Companies.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Use of Industry 4.0 technologies, agile organisations and adaptive technology-based business construct
-  Shorter payback models to de-risk future projects against any disruptive innovation
-  Long term contracts

## Selected Risk Factor 5 Pricing / Market Risk

| Category    | Probability   | Impact      |
|-------------|---------------|-------------|
| Credit      | Medium        | Medium      |
| Description | Description   | Description |

The investments that will be acquired by GGEF have not yet been identified. The activity of identifying, completing and realizing  attractive  investments  is  highly  competitive  and  involves  a  high  degree  of  uncertainty.  Since  GGEF  will compete with other investors for investments in Portfolio Companies, this may result in fewer attractive investment opportunities. In addition, such competition may have an adverse effect on the length of time required to fully invest the Fund.  To  the  extent  the  Fund  has  already  drawn  down  capital  from  Investors,  pending  investment  in  Portfolio Companies, these funds may be retained in cash or may be invested in Temporary Investments. Such investments may substantially reduce the Fund's overall return.

The Investment Manager believes that the Fund's investment program and research techniques moderate this risk through a careful selection of Portfolio Companies. No guarantee or representation, however, is made that the Fund's program will be successful, and investment results may vary substantially over time.

<!-- image -->

## F GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 68 OF 77

<!-- image -->

Many of the investments made by the Fund will be illiquid, and there can be no assurance that GGEF will be able to realize profits on its investments in a timely manner.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Either secure long term contracted cashflows to cover life of the project or invest in large market opportunity to mitigate demand risk
-  Invest in creating market leading solutions that are suitably priced
-  Carry out careful due diligence on potential Portfolio Investments

## Selected Risk Factor 6 Exit Risk

| Category   | Probability   | Impact   |
|------------|---------------|----------|
| Other      | Medium        | Medium   |

## Description

Most of the investments made by GGEF will be in unlisted companies whose securities should be considered illiquid. These investments may be difficult to value and to sell or otherwise liquidate, and the risk accompanying an investment in such companies is much greater than the risk of investing in publicly traded securities. Moreover, these unlisted companies are not regulated by the same disclosure and investment protection norms that apply to listed companies. Therefore,  the  Fund  may  make  investment  decisions  and  monitor  such  investments  on  the  basis  of  inadequate information in comparison to a publicly listed company.

It is expected that the companies in which GGEF invests will eventually either have their securities listed on an Indian stock exchange or such securities may be sold through a private sale or a merger / acquisition route (among other exit options that may be available), as a means of creating liquidity for that investment. However, there can be no assurance that the security may ever be disposed of or publicly traded. Furthermore, there can be no assurance that the listing of these securities will provide a viable exit mechanism, as these securities may suffer from low trading volumes and low market capitalization at the time of intended disposal. Besides, regulations / guidelines in certain jurisdictions generally impose  a  lock-in  period  on  promoters'  holdings  in  companies  seeking  listing  through  IPOs,  which  would  reduce secondary market liquidity. GGEF will endeavor to avoid or minimize such lock-ins on its shareholding in its Portfolio Companies to the extent possible. However, there can be no assurance that it will be in a position to do so.

GGEF may make investments which may not be advantageously disposed of prior to the date of dissolution of the Fund, either by expiration of the Fund's term or otherwise. Although the Investment Manager expects that investments will be disposed of prior to dissolution or be suitable for in kind distribution at dissolution and the Investment Manager has a limited ability to extend the term of the Fund, the Fund may have to sell, distribute, or otherwise dispose of investments at a disadvantageous time as a result of dissolution. In addition, although upon the dissolution of GGEF, the Investment Manager (or the relevant liquidator) will be required to use reasonable efforts to reduce to cash and cash equivalents such assets of the Fund as the Investment Manager or such liquidator deems it advisable to sell, there can be no assurance with respect to the time frame in which the winding-up and the final distribution of proceeds to the Investors will occur.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Rapid scale-up
-  Multiple Exits business construct
-  Control, pedigree of shareholders, appropriate leverage &amp; exit readiness

## Selected Risk Factor 7

## Government Regulatory Risk

| Category   | Probability   | Impact   |
|------------|---------------|----------|
| Sanctions  | Medium        | Medium   |

## Description

The value of the GGEF's investments may be adversely affected by potential political and social uncertainties in India. Certain  developments,  beyond  the  control  of  the  Fund,  such  as  the  possibility  of  nationalization,  expropriations,

<!-- image -->

## F GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 69 OF 77

<!-- image -->

confiscatory taxation, political changes, government regulation, social instability, diplomatic disputes or other similar developments, could adversely affect the Fund's investments.

India is a country which comprises diverse religious and ethnic groups. It is the world's most populous democracy. Ethnic  issues,  religious  conflicts  and  border  disputes  could  adversely  affect  economic  conditions  in  India  and consequently the Fund's investments.

While fiscal and legislative reforms have led to economic liberalization and stabilization in India over the past 10 years, the  possibility  that  these  reforms  may  be  halted  or  reversed  could  significantly  and  adversely  affect  the  value  of investments in India. GGEF's investments could also be adversely affected by changes in laws and regulations or the interpretation thereof, including those governing foreign investment, anti-inflationary measures, rates and methods of taxation, and restrictions on currency conversion, imports and sources of supplies.

Although India has experienced significant growth and is projected to undergo significant growth in the future, there can be no assurance that such growth will continue. Adverse economic conditions or stagnant economic development in India could adversely affect the value of the Fund's investments.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Invest in businesses that are/ will be commercially viable without subsidies
-  Align business construct to leverage robust regulatory support &amp; sustainability

## Selected Risk Factor 8 Partner Risk

| Category     | Probability   | Impact   |
|--------------|---------------|----------|
| Reputational | Medium        | Medium   |

Description

Instances of fraud and other deceptive practices committed by senior management of Portfolio Companies in which GGEF invests may undermine the Investment Manager's due diligence efforts with respect to such companies and, if such fraud is discovered, negatively affect the valuation of the GGEF's investments. In addition, when discovered, financial fraud may contribute to overall market volatility that can negatively impact the GGEF's investment program.

GGEF may be a minority investor and might not always be in a position to protect its interests effectively, particularly if management teams of Portfolio Companies pursue objectives which are inconsistent with those of the Fund. However, as a condition of investment in a Portfolio Company, GGEF expects that appropriate shareholder and supervisory rights will generally be negotiated to protect the its interests. GGEF will rely significantly on the existing management and boards of directors of such companies, which may include representatives of other investors with whom GGEF is not affiliated and whose interests or views may conflict with the views of the Fund.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Secure control / co-control across all investments
-  Partner with credible entities with strong ESG standards

## Selected Risk Factor 9 Foreign exchange risk

| Category   | Probability   | Impact   |
|------------|---------------|----------|
| Forex      | Medium        | Medium   |

Description

The currency of the Fund will be in INR. Investors will make a commitment to the Fund of an INR amount. Drawdowns by the Fund from its Investors shall be made pro rata to Total Commitments on such a Drawdown Date and will be an INR amount. A significant change in the currency exchange rate between the Indian Rupee and the currency of the jurisdiction of an investor may have a material impact on the overall commitment amount of such investor. The Fund will be under no obligation to engage in any hedging transactions.

Mitigation Measure(s)

<!-- image -->

-  Lock in capital cost and hedge currency
-  Drip feeding of capital into investments which acts as a natural hedge to the currency volatility

## Selected Risk Factor 10 Compliance (AML/CFT) risk

| Category     | Probability   | Impact   |
|--------------|---------------|----------|
| Reputational | Low           | Medium   |

## Description

Instances  of  fraud  and  other  deceptive  practices  committed  by  senior  management  of  the  Fund  or  its  portfolio companies which may undermine the Investment Manager's due diligence efforts with respect to such companies and, when such fraud is discovered, negatively affect the valuation of the Fund's investments. In addition, when discovered, financial fraud may contribute to overall market volatility that can negatively impact the Fund's investment program.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

-  Policies and procedures with respect to Compliance, AML etc. are in place to avoid these risks. These have been described in section B4 of the FP and in Annex 24
-  Strong risk management and governance structures followed by the Investment Manager which have been described in section B4 of the FP and in Annex 24
-  GGEF is regulated by the Securities Exchange Board of India (SEBI) regulated and follows SEBI's rules and guidelines which have been instituted to protect the interest of investors

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Executing Entity has adopted a comprehensive Environmental, Social and Governance Policy and put in place Environmental, Social and Governance Management System (ESGMS) for Green Growth Equity Fund, that has been designed  in  line  IFC  Performance  Standards  and  updated  as  per  request  of  AE  to  ensure  alignment  with  GCF Environmental and Social Policy, as well as Information Disclosure Policy and Indigenous People Policy 61 .

The ESGMS is presented under Annex 6 of this Funding Proposal. The ESGMS has been developed to ensure that the projects to be financed by the Programme will be designed and implemented in a sustainable manner.  It also ensures that GCF ESS policies are passed down from Accredited Entity to GGEF (Fund level) and to the projects financed by the GGEF (Investee level).

The ESGMS consists of following key parts:

At the core of GGEF's ESGMS is to identify potential environmental and social governance risks prior to investment disbursement and to institutionalize the monitoring mechanisms at an Investee level to supervise ESG performance during the term of the GGEF's investment.

As per ESGMS document, the following procedures are incorporated in GGEF's investment process to ensure that ESS risks are assessed and mitigated:

| ESG Integration in Investment Process                                                                             | ESG Integration in Investment Process                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Screening of project eligibility to GGEF's ESG Policy                                                             | The projects in the GGEF pipeline will be screened for 'eligibility' to GGEF's ESG Policy. In particular, project activities will be screened against GGEF ESGMS exclusion list to ensure that no activities under this list is financed. During this stage, the Executing Entity's team shall assess proposed investment activates to determine the environmental and social impacts and classify project environmental and social governance risk as per 'high', 'medium' and 'low' risk categories. Category A (High risk category): Activities with potential significant adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that, individually or cumulatively, are diverse, irreversible, or unprecedented. Category B (Medium risk category). Activities with potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that individually or cumulatively, are few, generally site- specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures; and Category C (Low risk category). Activities with minimal or no adverse environmental and/or social risks and/or impacts. The resulting category for a project (s) to be financed by the GGEF shall further inform the |
| Conduct environmental and social governance due diligence (ESGDD) of projects that passed initial screening stage | During ESGDD stage, the Executing Entity's team shall assess potential ESG risks of proposed project as well as environmental and social governance practiced by the potential investee company prior to the GGEF's investment. GGEF shall identify appropriate mitigation measures (if any required) in Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP); by also specifying Investee companies' responsibilities and timeline for implementing preventive, mitigation and compensation measures in ESAP.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Incorporating Investee company's obligation to institutionalize                                                   | The ESAP developed during the due-diligence stage will be part of shareholder agreement between GGEF and the respective investee company, therefore the investee will be legally required to implement ESAP.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

61 The Executing Entity with the guidance of Accredited Entity has prepared Resettlement Policy Framework and Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) in line with GCF ESS requirements, as well as updated its information disclosure requirement to match requirements of GCF. Please refer to Annex 6 for details. The Exclusion List has been updated to include GCF ESS, as well as other IFI's (e.g. FMOs) requirements.

| Environmental and Social Governance into legal agreements                               |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Post investment, regular monitoring and reporting of Investee Company's ESG performance | Post investment, the Investee companies will be monitored on a [quarterly] basis by the Programme Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) Head Officer for projects ESG performance against the ESAP. The monitoring will involve site visits, regular communication with investee company management team and submission of reports. It is noteworthy, that at the investee level, the companies will be required to institutionalize ESG governance as per GGEF ESG policy requirement-so the GGEF will directly promote institutionalization of good practice environmental and social governance in India and will set a positive demonstrative effect for other companies within the industry. |

The Investment Manager will report on an annual basis on ESG performance of the fund and its portfolio companies to investors, including GCF. For additional details on environmental and social governance, please refer to Annex 6 .

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Executing Entity has performed a programme level gender assessment to identify gender specific vulnerabilities and risks in India and sectors targeted for investment by the Programme. The detailed assessment is included in Annex 8 of this FP, the key vulnerabilities are summarized below:

- Lack of inclusive design in planning for infrastructure projects, that ensures that infrastructure works for everyone including women and disadvantaged groups. It is noteworthy, that stakeholder consultations in India is often limited to engagement of woman and disadvantaged groups which results in non-inclusive project design.
- Lack  of  gender  inclusive  policies  in  private  sector  companies,  resulting  in  gender  gaps  in  employment  and operationalization of infrastructure projects.
- Disproportional access to resources, skills and education by different gender groups.

Based on this assessment, the Executing Entity has made its commitment to mainstream gender considerations in its investment decision making and address gender gaps at the Fund level and at the level of projects financed by it (investee level).

The Executing Entity shall update the current Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan on an annual basis and incorporate inputs from the portfolio companies starting two years from commencing implementation. This shall explore including action plans for wider benefits and priorities of the programme in relation to the potential gender-sensitive development impacts.

Specifically, the executing entity will implement following actions:

62 The resulting E&amp;S category of the project to be financed by the GGEF shall also determine the institutional requirements for disclosure in accordance with GGEF ESGMS, which has been updated to reflect GCF Information Disclosure Policy.

The Programme level Gender Action Plan is included in Annex 8 of this document and has been designed to ensure alignment to GCF Gender Policy. The progress on GAP implementation will be actively monitored by the Accredited Entity and reported to GCF as part of APRs.

The relevant gender-related outcome as a result of Programme level GAP implementation includes:

- Deeper mainstreaming of gender considerations into investment decision making and when planning for green infrastructure projects.
- Building relevant competence and capacity both at the Fund and Investee level to implement Gender Action Plan.

A better understanding of the relevance of gender in green infrastructure investments, as GGEF plans to contribute to knowledge sharing and dissemination activities both to key programme stakeholders as community. Furthermore, the GGEF's portfolio companies are likely to create positive demonstrative impact in the industry by incorporating genderinclusive planning for green infrastructure projects.

## G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

After the investment approval, the main function of the Investment Manager (EE) is to monitor portfolio performance against  targets  undertaken  during  investment  stage  (this  includes  financial,  climate,  SDG  and  ESS  impacts).  The monitoring is undertaken by full-time investment team, who sit on Boards of individual portfolio companies and receive on-going  updates  from  portfolio  company  through  direct  communication  with  management  team,  site  visits  and reporting.

The team works closely with the portfolio companies and receives detailed management reports with relevant metrics on a regular basis. Once a month the investment team meets to discuss updates / progress within the portfolio, as well as any potential areas of risk within the portfolio companies, review of financial performance and overall governance aspects of the  investments. Appropriate strategies and next steps are  then agreed upon  and  implemented by the respective teams. There is also a comprehensive quarterly review of each portfolio company.

Portfolio  performance  is  monitored  through  building  collaborative  relationship  with  management  and  reporting  as follows:

- Monthly reporting from portfolio companies
- News tracking
- Periodic Board Meetings and on-going communication with management team to build company's value.
- Site visits
- Ensuring compliance with shareholders agreement
- Performance reviews by the Investment Manager investment team against targets (both commercial and impact related).

Based on above monitoring process, the Investment Manager submits following reports to GGEF investors:

- Quarterly reports on Fund performance (which shall include information on individual company performance)
- Annual audited statements
- Annual ESS report
- Annual climate and sustainability impact report

The Investment Manager applies fiduciary duties as per PE industry best practices: It reports on Fund Performance quarterly  and  annually  to  Limited  Partners  (investors).  The  audit  of  annual  finance  statements  is  performed  by independent provider, to ensure transparency and control of flow of funds. Impact reporting will be performed annually in  house, with 2 GCF specific programme independent evaluation to be undertaken by AE and shared with GCF. Environmental and Social Governance is carried out as per GCF ESS Policy and IFC Performance Standards.

## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

- [x] ☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

- [ ] full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy, and

- [ ] redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP164

Proposal name:

Green Growth Equity Fund (GGEF)

Accredited entity:

Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMO)

Project/programme size:

Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1.1 Impact potential

Scale: High

1. India is the seventh-largest country in the world. Located in South Asia, its land area extends from the heights of the Himalayas in the north to the tropical rain forests of the south. The country has reported significant economic growth, reaching an annualized rate of 7.5 per cent in the late 2000s. With its 1.5 billion people, India is regarded as the world's third-largest economy in terms of purchasing power parity. However, 30 per cent of its population remains below poverty line.
2. India ranks seventh in the Global Climate Risk Index. 1  The average temperature across India increased by about 0.7 °C between 1901 and 2018, largely due to anthropogenic, greenhouse gas (GHG), emission-induced warming. The temperature is projected to continue to increase by about 2.4-4.7 °C by 2100. 2  Extreme weather events and natural hazards are projected to increase in frequency and severity in the coming decades. India could lose 1.8 per cent of its gross domestic product (GDP) by 2050 unless effective mitigation and adaptation measures are implemented swiftly.
3. India is the third-largest GHG emitter in the world contributing to 7 per cent of global emissions. In 2014, the country emitted 2,607.49 MtCO2eq of GHG from the energy, industry, agriculture, and waste sectors, with the energy and agriculture sectors combined accounting for 2,326.2 MtCO2eq, or 89 per cent of the country's total emissions. The land use, land-use change, and forestry (LULUCF) sector remained a net sink. Taking into account removals from LULUCF, the country's net emissions were estimated to be 2,306.3 MtCO2eq in 2014. The increase continued, and India's total GHG emissions in 2018 reached 2,993 MtCO2eq, excluding LULUCF. Despite the Government's ambitious policy targets, compared with the values for 2010, the country's GHG emissions are estimated to have increased by 46-50 per cent by 2020, and are projected to rise by more than 100 per cent by 2030.
4. In order to deliver its commitments in terms of its nationally determined contributions (NDCs), India requires additional financial, technological and capacity-building support. The country achieved a reduction in emission intensity of GDP by 21 per cent over the period 20052014. However, the total investment required is estimated at USD 2.5 trillion for the period

1  https://germanwatch.org/sites/germanwatch.org/files/Global%20Climate%20Risk%20Index%202021\_1.pdf

at &lt;https://www.springer.com/gp/book/9789811543265&gt;.

2  Ministry of Earth Sciences. 2020. Assessment of Climate Change over the Indian Region , chapter 2, table 2.9. Available

<!-- image -->

- 2016-2030. 3  According to a forthcoming study by Climate Policy Initiative, India is mobilizing less than 25 per cent of the investments needed to reach the target.
5. The mitigation and adaptation gaps are especially evident in the infrastructure sector. Although the country is in the need of annual infrastructure investment equivalent to 7-8 per cent of GDP (about USD 5 trillion) by 2030, the actual investment remains limited to 4-5  per cent of GDP due to government budgetary constraints, and the country's limited capacity to structure and deliver climate change infrastructure projects.
6. The proposed mitigation programme aims to mobilize public and private sector funding to capitalize Green Growth Feeder Fund Pte Ltd (GGFF) to provide funding in a form of equity for the Green Growth Equity Fund (GGEF) to invest in climate change mitigation projects in India. The sectors targeted for investments by GGEF include renewable energy, transport, resource efficiency (water and waste), and energy services.
7. GGEF was created in 2018, with anchor investment from the National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF) of India and from the Government of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, to invest in green infrastructure aiming to decarbonize the economy and reduce climate change impacts in India. It is registered as an alternative investment fund in Mumbai, India, and managed by EverSource Capital Pvt Ltd (EverSource).
8. GGFF is a fund created in Singapore to pool capital from international investors to invest in GGEF. GGFF is managed by Everstone Capital Asia Pte Ltd.
9. The accredited entity (AE) is the Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMO), and the executing entity (EE) is EverSource.
10. The programme aims to raise capital from GCF, the AE, and other public and private sector investors to capitalize GGEF up to USD 945 million. GCF is requested to provide USD 132.5 million in equity and a USD 4.5 million grant.
11. Investments in GGFF are categorized as either class A or class B shares. The class B share that GCF is to invest USD 132.5 million in is structured to subordinate as first-loss capital protection to reduce the commercial risks of class A shares, in which international investors (both public and private institutions) will be  invited to invest.
12. Component 1 entails an equity investment by GGFF into GGEF for further investments into 6-8 infrastructure projects in renewable energy, e-mobility, energy services, and resource efficiency projects. These projects are to be commercially viable and with strong climate change mitigation impacts and innovation potential. Component 2 involves technical assistance to promote capacity-building, and knowledge transfer with respect to low-carbon technologies and business models promoted by GGEF in the targeted sectors. It also aims to produce studies and policy recommendations to support policy dialogue and regulatory framework development in India, taking into account inputs received during seminars and workshops with public and private sector stakeholders. The assistance will further enhance the capacity of infrastructure companies that GGEF invests in to assess climate impacts. It will support the creation of standards and mechanisms for the monitoring, reporting and verification of GHG emissions.
13. With the GCF investment of USD 132.5 million as first-loss capital, GGFF is expected to mobilize USD397.5 million from international public and private sector institutions to secure USD 530 million in total funding to be invested in GGEF. Additional to GGFF investment, GGEF will secure USD 410 million, which includes a USD 340 million commitment from its anchor

3  Climate Policy Initiative, 2020. More up-to-date data by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) indicates a need

2017).

of USD 3.1 trillion climate investment for India in key sectors between 2018 and 2030 to fully meet its NDCs (IFC,

<!-- image -->

- investors (NIIF, and the Foreign, Commonwealth &amp; Development Office of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland). In total, GGEF will mobilize USD 940 million for its investments in climate change mitigation infrastructure companies in India.
14. The AE estimates that GGEF investments in the infrastructure companies will attract coinvestments of USD 890 million from other investors. As the infrastructure companies would be funded with bank debts of USD 2,900 million, the total amount of climate change mitigation finance that GGEF will mobilizes could reach USD 4,730 million (USD 1,830 million equity plus USD 2,900 million debt).
15. The AE has provided a list of the infrastructure companies that GGEF has identified for investment. The list includes companies in utility-scale and distributed renewable energy, electric bus transportation, and waste management. In addition, the deal pipeline includes a battery service company for small electric vehicles, energy efficiency companies, and water and waste management companies.
16. According to the projection by the AE, GGEF investments in utility-scale and distributed renewable energy will represent 33 per cent of the portfolio, followed by electric vehicle transportation (e-mobility) (13 per cent), and water and wastewater management (12 per cent). Other sectors such as waste management, battery-charging infrastructure, and energy efficiency will each represent 10-11 per cent of the GGEF portfolio.
17. The AE estimates that GGEF will achieve 480 GWh of electricity supply from renewable sources from a renewable energy generation capacity of 111.9 GWp and through waste energy, water desalination and sewage treatment plants over their useful life of 25 years. The GGEF financial support for e-mobility will lead to the deployment of 1,025 electric buses, 150,000 electric 3-wheelers and 50,000 electric 2-wheelers that are projected to drive 50,133 million kilometres in total during a 10-year lifetime. The volume of water and wastewater that will be treated is estimated at 6,062 million m 3 .
18. The AE expects GGEF to reduce GHG emissions by 452 MtCO2eq in total across the lifetime of the facilities that GGEF finances. An additional 20,000 jobs will be generated.
19. The independent TAP is of the view that impact potential of the programme is high.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale:  Medium/High

## 1.2.1. Potential for scaling up and replication

20. A funding modality such as GGEF can be effective in mobilizing offshore funding as its technical and operational risks of its investments in the nascent sector are mitigated. The GCF equity contribution for first-loss capital protection will enable GGFF to attract USD 397.5 million from offshore investors at a co-financing ratio of 1:3 to invest in GGEF. However, the potential for scaling up and replicability of this type funding modality is dependent on the availability of concessional funding that could effectively mitigate a part of the risks that offshore investors are hesitant to assume.
21. GGEF will implement climate change mitigation investments in the infrastructure sector in India, where climate change mitigation technologies and operations remain uncommon. GGEF investments are expected to showcase and demonstrate the technical and commercial feasibility of climate mitigation interventions. With the involvement of co-investors and banks, GGEF interventions could be replicated in the market. Additional funding and investments could be attracted to promote climate change mitigations in the GGEF-targeted infrastructure sector of renewable energy, energy efficiency, transport, water and wastewater, and waste management.

<!-- image -->

## 1.2.2. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

22. GGEF will implement investments in the target sector of renewable energy, energy efficiency, transport, water and wastewater, and waste management, where climate change mitigation interventions remain nascent in India. The technologies and business models that GGEF aims to implement are new and innovative. Therefore, GGEF investments will be demonstrational. Participating in the investments, co-investors and banks would be exposed to innovative climate change mitigation technologies and approaches, which will then help to disseminate knowledge among market players and stakeholders. Therefore, GGEF will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment in India.

## 1.2.3. Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

23. GGEF climate change mitigation interventions are in line with the government policies and strategies indicated in NDCs and National Action Plan on Climate Change. The technologies and business models that GGEF will introduce with its investments manifest the technical and commercial viability of climate change mitigation investments. The programme includes technical assistance to produce studies and policy recommendations to enhance policy dialogue and contribute to the development of a regulatory framework favourable to climate change mitigation interventions in India. Nevertheless, the details of the AE strategy and implementation in this regard remain to be developed.
24. The independent TAP considers the paradigm potential of the programme as medium/high.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

25. The programme will contribute to the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 6, Ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all; SDG 7, Ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all; SDG 9, Build resilient infrastructure, promote inclusive and sustainable industrialization and foster innovation; SDG 11, Make cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable; SDG 12, Ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns; and SDG 13, Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

## 1.3.1. Environment co-benefits

26. GGEF aims to invest in 6-8 climate change mitigation infrastructure companies in the targeted sectors of renewable energy, transport, resource efficiency (water and waste), and energy services in India. The programme will contribute to improving the environment with sustainable supply and provision of renewable energy, water and wastewater services, transportation, and waste management. The AE projects that the GGEF financial support for emobility will lead to the deployment of 1,025 electric buses, 150,000 electric 3-wheelers, and 50,000 electric 2-wheelers, which will enable savings of 500 million tons of diesel and 1,500 million tons of compressed natural gas.
27. The AE further assumes that GGEF investment in water and waste treatment projects would benefit more than 40 million people.

## 1.3.2. Economic co-benefits

28. The AE estimates that GGEF will build up a renewable energy generation capacity of 111.9 GWp. The GGEF financial support for e-mobility is assumed to lead to the deployment of

<!-- image -->

- 1,025 electric buses, 150,000 electric 3-wheelers, and 50,000 electric 2-wheelers that are expected to drive 50,133 million kilometres in total. In addition, 20,000 new jobs will be created as a result of GGEF investments.
29. Additional energy generation capacity to be realized with GGEF investment will help the country reduce its dependence on energy imports. According to the estimation by the AE, GGEF investment in utility-scale renewable energy generation will free up USD 686 million of foreign exchange annually towards alternate uses.

## 1.3.3. Gender-sensitive development impact

30. The AE claims that a secondary contribution of GGEF will be towards gender equality (SDG 5) and decent work and economic growth (SDG 8) through operations of all of its investee companies. In this connection, the AE has submitted as an annex to the funding proposal 'Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan for Green Growth Equity Fund', which was prepared anticipating GGEF application for GCF investment. While it has identified actions to be taken by GGEF, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) notes that the gender action plan remains in the preliminary stage with respect to GGEF policy and targets to promote gender-sensitive issues at both GGEF level and investee company level.
31. The TAP regards the project's sustainable development potential as high.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

## 1.4.1. Economic and social development level of the country and the population affected

32. The World Bank classifies India as a lower-middle-income country. About 28 per cent of its population, or 364 million people, are reported to live below the national poverty line of USD 1.90 per person per day. About 1.77 million people are homeless, and 4.9 per cent of the population aged 15 years and above are unemployed.
33. In India, per capita electricity consumption remains as low as one third of the world's average, or 917 kWh. The World Resources Institute lists the country as one of the 17 'Extremely High Baseline Water Stress' countries in the National Water Stress Ranking. 4  Many households have inadequate provision of basic infrastructure and services, such as access to electricity, safe water, and mobility.
34. Climate change is impacting the livelihoods of the population in India, especially of the most vulnerable. India's average temperature has increased by about 0.7 °C since 1901. It is projected to increase further by about 2.4-4.7 °C, causing extreme weather events and natural hazards that will put pressure on the country's natural resources, such as water, damage infrastructure, and threaten the livelihoods of its population, especially of the most vulnerable. The country has experienced changes in monsoonal patterns, prolonged droughts, flooding, and changes in sea level. By 2050, the country could lose the equivalent of 1.8 per cent of its GDP.

## 1.4.2. Absence of alternative sources of financing

35. GCF is requested to provide USD135 million in funding for GGFF to support GGEF investments in infrastructure companies in the targeted sectors of renewable energy, transport, resource efficiency (water and waste), and energy services. The GCF funding will be the first-

17 Countries, Home to One-Quarter of the World's Population, Face Extremely

.  Available at &lt;https://www.wri.org/blog/2019/08/17-countries-home-one-quarter-world-

4  Hofste RW, Reig P and Schleifer L. 2019.

High Water Stress population-face-extremely-high-water-stress&gt;.

<!-- image -->

- loss capital protection to reduce the commercial risks of investments undertaken by international investors, both public and private sector institutions, in GGFF.
36. Since the initial financial close of GGEF in 2018, EverSource, the EE, has marketed GGEF to more than 190 potential investors, but with limited success. Concerns raised by potential investors have included issues about EE being a first-time fund manager and newly created joint venture, and about investment risks in infrastructure in India in general. Investors have also been concerned about the GGEF focus on direct investments rather than fund investments. Moreover, some have limited appetite for infrastructure investments in emerging markets. Although a few investors did indicate a preliminary interest in GGEF, further discussion slowed as investors' priorities shifted to assessing the impacts of COVID-19 on their existing portfolio rather than exploring new investments.
37. The response by the AE to the TAP has confirmed that the GCF first-loss capital projection can be effective in attracting potential investors to reconsider investments in GGEF. With GCF first-loss capital protection, the EE has been able to re-approach and secure interest from 15 investors, including the AE.
38. The GCF first-loss capital protection is structured to enable international investors in GGFF to secure their return and investments up to USD 132.5 million. International investors are protected by GCF for about one third of their investments of USD 397.5 million in GGFF.
39. The TAP considers that alternate sources of financing to provide a similar level of firstloss capital protection to that provided by GCF are unavailable.

## 1.4.3. Need to strengthen institutions and implementing capacity

40. Climate-resilient and sustainable infrastructure investments remain at a nascent stage in India. The sector is facing a shortage not only of capital but also operational expertise and governance standards, as well as innovative business models. Critical technologies required to facilitate low-carbon transition, such as energy storage or electric-vehicle-charging infrastructure, have yet to be developed in India.
41. The aim of the GCF technical assistance grant will be to develop technical and operational capacity, chiefly that of the EE and investee infrastructure companies. The programme will provide support for building local sectoral expertise through the preparation of feasibility studies, local/international collaborations, training, and knowledge transfers in both the public and private sectors. The technical assistance is expected to assess and promote effective policy dialogue and regulatory frameworks to make them favourable for the private sector to invest in climate change mitigation interventions in the targeted infrastructure sector in India.
42. The TAP views the needs of the recipient as high.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: Medium/High

## 1.5.1. Existence of a national climate strategy

43. India submitted its intended nationally determined contributions (INDCs) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change in 2015, with a commitment to reduce the emission intensity of its GDP by 33-35 per cent by 2030, below the 2005 level. India has committed to increasing the share of non-fossil-based energy resources to 40 per cent of installed electric power capacity by 2030, with the help of transfer of technology and low-cost international finance, including from GCF. Regarding adaptation, the INDCs state the country's aim to better adapt to climate change by enhancing investments in sectors vulnerable to climate

<!-- image -->

- change, especially: agriculture and water resources; the Himalayan region; coastal regions; health; and disaster management. The climate change interventions that the country is determined to continue include: promotion of renewable energy generation; reduction in emissions from the transportation sector; promotion of energy efficiency; reduction in emissions from waste; and development of climate-resilient infrastructure.

## 1.5.2. Coherence with existing policies

44. The National Action Plan on Climate Change (adopted 30 June 2008) outlines steps to simultaneously advance India's development and climate change-related objectives of adaptation and mitigation. It lists eight national missions on climate change: National Solar Energy; National Mission for Enhancing Energy Efficiency; National Mission on Sustainable Habitat; National Water Mission; National Mission for Sustaining the Himalayan Ecosystem; National Mission for a Green India; National Mission for Sustainable Agriculture; and National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change. The National Solar Mission promotes the development and use of solar energy for power generation and other uses, with the ultimate objective of making solar competitive with fossil-based energy options. The National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency promotes energy consumption decreases in large, energyconsuming industries. The National Mission on Sustainable Habitat promotes energy efficiency as a core component of urban planning. The National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change focuses on enhancement of better understanding of climate science, impacts and challenges. It also encourages private-sector initiatives to develop adaptation and mitigation technologies through venture capital funds.

## 1.5.3. Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

45. The AE is a Netherlands entrepreneurial development bank founded in 1970. It was granted a banking licence by the central bank of the Netherlands and is rated AAA by Fitch Ratings and Standard &amp; Poor's. It has operated in developing countries for more than 50 years, with a current portfolio of about USD 12.6 billion. The AE has built its exposure to private equity of USD 4.1 billion through direct and indirect investments. It has been funding and supporting projects in India across agriculture, food and water, financial institutions, and energy. Currently, its exposure to India exceeds USD 965.3 million.
46. EverSource, the EE, was selected to be the investment manager for GGEF by NIIF and DFID. In April 2017, India's Ministry of Finance announced establishment of GGEF with the commitment by the Government of India and by the Government of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland to anchor invest up to GBP120 million (approximately USD 167.2 million) each under the NIIF framework. The selection process started from prequalification in 2017, and concluded by selecting EverSource as investment manager in 2018. Then, an initial financial closure with a commitment of USD 340 million was reached.
47. EverSource is a joint venture between Everstone Group (Everstone) and Lightsource bp Renewable Energy Investments Limited (Lightsource bp). Everstone is a private equity and real estate investment firm, with over USD 5 billion of assets across 70 investments in private equity and infrastructure in India.
48. Established in 2010, Lightsource bp (then Lightsource Renewable Energy) is a private company in the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland focusing on the development, acquisition and management of large-scale solar projects. In 2017, it was renamed Lightsource bp, being an equal partnership with BP. It currently has a global portfolio of 3 GW of solar projects, including a 60 MW project owned through GGEF in Maharashtra, India.

<!-- image -->

49. GGEF is the first fund managed by EverSource. Therefore, the capacity of EverSource to manage GGEF and investments in the target sectors remains to be demonstrated. In the discussion with the TAP, the AE clarified that its due diligence report of EverSource's operational capacity was not ready for GCF review.

## 1.5.4. Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

50. As GGEF will invest in various infrastructure companies during its lifetime, the stakeholder engagement with respect to each investee company/project will be conducted at the time of the investment.
51. The AE and EE have had regular discussions with India's Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change, and with the national designated authority of GCF to provide information and seek inputs with respect to the programme.
52. A no-objection letter has been issued by the national designated authority and is attached to the funding proposal.
53. The TAP considers country ownership of the programme to be medium/high.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: High

## 1.6.1. Cost-effectiveness and efficiency regarding financial and non-financial aspects

54. According to the estimation by the AE, GGEF will achieve GHG emission reductions of 452 MtCO2eq in total across the lifetime of the facilities (25 years at most). The cost of GHG reductions against GCF financing of USD 137 million is estimated to be USD 0.3/tCO2eq.

## 1.6.2. Amount of co-financing

55. With GCF investment of USD 137 million (including a USD 4.5 million technical assistance grant), GGFF is expected to raise USD 397.5 million from international public and private sector institutions to secure USD 530 million in total funding to be invested in GGEF. Accordingly, the co-financing ratio is 1:2.9. If the technical assistance grant is excluded, then the ratio would be 1:3.
56. At the GGEF level, GGFF investment is to be matched with a USD 410 million investment from anchor sponsors and new investors. At the investee company level, GGEF investment will attract co-investment and also mobilizes bank loans. In total, USD 4.7 billion in funding is to be mobilized to the target sectors. Therefore, the GCF investment of USD 137 million is expected to attract about USD 4.57 billion in financing from other sources.

## 1.6.3. Programme/project financial viability and other financial indicators

57. The GGEF climate change mitigation investments in the infrastructure sector will be undertaken only if the investments are assessed as being commercially viable. The assessment will be critical to also securing funding from co-investors and banks.
58. According to the AE estimation by the AE, class A (international investors) and class B (GCF) investments in GGFF will achieve a net internal rate of return of 15.5 per cent and 11.3 per cent, respectively. GCF is expected to have its USD 132.5 million investment returned by the end of the fund's life.

<!-- image -->

## 1.6.4. Industry best practices

59. GGEF aims to introduce innovative climate change mitigation technologies and business models with its investments in the target sectors of renewable energy, energy efficiency, transport, water and wastewater, and waste management in India. The programme will include technical assistance activities to enhance capacity-building of investee companies about climate change mitigation technologies and operations. It will further support the creation of standards and mechanisms for monitoring, reporting and verification of GHG emissions of the investees.
60. The TAP views the efficiency and effectiveness of the programme as being high.

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

61. The TAP recommends that the Board endorse the proposed project as presented.

<!-- image -->

## Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings (FP164)

Proposal name:

Green Growth Equity Fund

Accredited entity:

Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMO)

## Impact Potential

The High rating is noted.

The AE concurs with the ITAP's assessment. EverSource Capital shall continue to work through an owneroperator model to ensure strong implementation across its target sectors to create impact commensurate to  its  potential.  Identified  climate  impact  and  identified  metrics  shall  be  leading  indicators  for  the performance of GGEF's portfolio companies.

## Paradigm shift potential

The Medium / High rating is noted.

EverSource  Capital's  control  approach  to  investing  and  buy  or  build  (where  necessary)  strategy  was adopted to ensure that capital was employed in high impact sectors in the most efficient manner.

The build strategy has been demonstrated through GGEF's platforms - Radiance Renewables and GreenCell Mobility, where in the absence of investible platforms to acquire, EverSource took time to build-out a credible platform from scratch, that can now absorb large amounts of institutional equity/debt capital and scale the impact potential. The strategy to drip feed capital during the buildout stage to ensure the highest capital efficiency and investment in commercially viable projects, was useful for its platform companies to ride out the Covid-19 storm.

GCF's Component 2 will further substantiate GGEF's build strategy as it looks further to invest in nascent sectors  (energy  storage,  carbon  capture,  etc.)  where  investible  platforms  need  to  be  created.  The Technical Assistance (TA) will be important for capability enhancement, establishing proof of concept / viability before scale and developing suitable frameworks for impact assessment and monitoring.

With  focus  on  achieving  scale  right  from  the  beginning,  creating  an  ecosystem  (financial,  regulatory, policy,  etc.  support)  for  its  portfolio  companies  to  mature  and  continuous  engagement  with  relevant stakeholders; GGEF shall continue to work towards making climate investments an attractive asset class for public and private capital.

EverSource Capital and the AE have already developed the TA Eligibility Criteria and will be developing a detailed and robust TA Operations Manual to ensure efficacy of use of the TA grant capital.

## Sustainable Development potential

The High rating is noted.

By virtue of GGEF's mandate to invest in climate positive sectors with highest ESG and HSE standards, all GGEF's target sectors overlap directly with specific SDGs as identified by ITAP. Further, the environmental, economic and social co-benefits of GGEF's projects shall cover nearly all of the SDGs.

The ITAP's observation on Gender Action Plan is well noted. While Gender Assessment and Action Plan

<!-- image -->

are an important part of the E&amp;S guidelines of each portfolio company right from the diligence during acquisition stage, especially for the platforms incubated by GGEF, the Gender Action Plan shall become a bigger focal point now that the platforms have achieved significant scale. For example, GreenCell Mobility is  targeting  a  minimum number of women on its workforce. The gender positive work culture trickles downwards  from  EverSource  Capital  -  where  the  women  form  majority  of  the  workforce,  to  GGEF's portfolio companies.

Further,  GGEF  has  conducted  a  comprehensive  Gender  Assessment  and  developed  Action  Plan  at  the Fund level as well as at the portfolio level. As required by GCF, it will be a live document and updated on annual basis. Moreover, GGEF will conduct training and capacity building sessions as well as participate in thought leadership events, as appropriate. Annual monitoring shall be conducted to monitor progress on the action plan.

## Needs of the Recipient

The High rating is noted.

India's  commitment  to  climate  action  is  driven  by  the  recognition  of  its  vulnerability  to  the  impact  of climate change and by virtue of its demography and geography. The World Bank estimates that climate change  shall  impact  Indian  GDP  per  capita  by  ~10%  by  2050 1 .  Interest  in  climate  action  has  further spiked  during  the  Covid-19  pandemic.  Early  action  (policy  and  regulatory  tailwinds)  has  attracted substantial  private  and  public  capital  towards  renewables,  enabling  India  to  be  well  on  its  way  to decarbonize its energy generation and meet its goals from the Paris Agreement.

For deeper impact, there is however a gap in the available avenues for investment through a multisector, comprehensive  approach  focused  on  climate  mitigation  and  adaptation  to  generate  a  truly  material positive climate impact. This is the gap that GGEF hopes to plug in.

## Country Ownership

The Medium / High rating is noted.

As  highlighted  by  the  ITAP,  India  has  a  long-standing  commitment  towards  climate  action  through  its Paris Agreement Goals, climate mitigation strategies, engagement with global and local stakeholders, etc.

GGEF is also anchored with the agenda to crowd in public and private capital towards climate positive investments.  Across  its  target  sectors,  EverSource  Capital  continues  to  interact  with  government stakeholders, private entities, etc. who have shown support for GGEF's projects.

EverSource Capital was selected the Investment Manager for GGEF through a global procurement process that  found the skills of a global renewables business in congruence with a leading private equity fund manager to be an ideal combination to manage this first of its kind climate focused fund in India. Time and again the unique combination of the Sponsors' (Everstone and Lightsource bp) networks have helped GGEF  and  its  portfolio  companies  in  global  procurement  (especially  for  the  renewables  businesses), business  development,  identification  of  talent  and  securing  financing  at  favourable  terms.  While EverSource  Capital  is  a  first-time  fund  manager,  senior  individuals  from  the  Investment  Team  have previously worked with each in different roles and capacities.

## Efficiency and Effectiveness

The High rating is noted.

The AE concurs with ITAP's assessment.

1  Source : World Bank Research, Mint (July 2019)

<!-- image -->

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

We/AE would like to thank the ITAP for recognizing the high impact potential of this programme, its strong alignment towards sustainable development and high efficiency and effectiveness through a implementation driven framework which would lead to high responsiveness to the needs of the recipients.
